I'm just restless, I guess, not really thoughtless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness and bouncing between tasks is causing you a lot of distress or getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is getting in the way. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and over at the community center too. \n\nMy husband, and the other volunteers I help out with... well, they just can't really count on me to keep plans straight anymore. Not without someone checking behind me to make sure things are actually getting done. So, yes, it's definitely causing some frustration for all of us.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice this restlessness and difficulty staying focused on one task?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it's been especially noticeable for about... oh, a year and ten months now. That was right around the time I took on the extra coordinating duties at the community center, on top of all my other activities. It just really seemed to stand out after that.\n\nDoctor: Was there anything significant happening in your life around the time you took on the extra duties at the community center, aside from the new responsibilities themselves?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Let me think... no, nothing out of the ordinary. It was just the usual day-to-day routine for my husband and me. Taking on those extra duties was really the only big change right around then. Everything else was pretty much exactly the same as always.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's actually quite bothersome. Like, if I'm waiting in line at the pharmacy, or even just waiting my turn to speak at one of our volunteer meetings, I get so fidgety. I'll catch myself sighing out loud, or... well, just jumping in and interrupting before the other person is finished. It's hard to just stand there and wait.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty staying focused at home, or is it mainly when you're involved in activities outside the home?\n\nPatient: It happens in both places, honestly. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband... well, my husband and the other volunteers have found they just can’t really count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me. So, yes, it's definitely an issue at the house just as much as when I'm out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Well, not running or climbing, no. I'm a bit past the age for that sort of thing. But I do get very restless. If I have to sit still for too long, I get this... stirred-up feeling inside. It's like I just suddenly need to stand up and straighten something out, or go check on something around the house. I just can't seem to stay put in one spot.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. My kitchen table is actually a pretty good example of that. It's just... well, it's covered in piles right now. I have a pile for my dance class, one for knitting, another for garden events, and then paperwork for trips we're taking. They're just all over the table, and when I sit down to go through them, I... I can’t always figure out what needs to be done first. It's just hard to get it all in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. For example, when I help out with the paperwork at the community center... I'll miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I'll write down the wrong room time on a note. Everything else was pretty much exactly the same as always.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's actually quite bothersome. Like, if I'm waiting in line at the pharmacy, or even just waiting my turn to speak at one of our volunteer meetings, I get so fidgety. I'll catch myself sighing out loud, or... well, just jumping in and interrupting before the other person is finished. It's hard to just stand there and wait.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty staying focused at home, or is it mainly when you're involved in activities outside the home?\n\nPatient: It happens in both places, honestly. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband... well, my husband and the other volunteers have found they just can’t really count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me. So, yes, it's definitely an issue at the house just as much as when I'm out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Well, not running or climbing, no. I'm a bit past the age for that sort of thing. But I do get very restless. If I have to sit still for too long, I get this... stirred-up feeling inside. It's like I just suddenly need to stand up and straighten something out, or go check on something around the house. I just can't seem to stay put in one spot.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. My kitchen table is actually a pretty good example of that. It's just... well, it's covered in piles right now. I have a pile for my dance class, one for knitting, another for garden events, and then paperwork for trips we're taking. They're just all over the table, and when I sit down to go through them, I... I can’t always figure out what needs to be done first. It's just hard to get it all in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. For example, when I help out with the paperwork at the community center... I'll miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I'll write down the wrong room time on a note. And the thing is, I always think I've checked it carefully, but the mistakes still happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as anxiety or depression, alongside these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really felt depressed or overly anxious. It's just the trouble keeping things straight and feeling a bit restless, like I mentioned. But as far as my mood goes, I generally feel fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I made the appointment because I just feel like I’m always up and moving. Even on a quiet day when I don't have much going on, I'm just bouncing around. \n\nI'll go from checking my garden club list, over to my knitting bag, and then I'm looking at travel papers... usually before I’ve really finished any one of those things. It just feels constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I do that. I might bounce around from task to task, but I don't really do things on impulse. I'm just restless, I guess, not really thoughtless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness and bouncing between tasks is causing you a lot of distress or getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really is getting in the way. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and over at the community center too. \n\nMy husband, and the other volunteers I help out with... well, they just can't really count on me to keep plans straight anymore. Not without someone checking behind me to make sure things are actually getting done. So, yes, it's definitely causing some frustration for all of us.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice this restlessness and difficulty staying focused on one task?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it's been especially noticeable for about... oh, a year and ten months now. That was right around the time I took on the extra coordinating duties at the community center, on top of all my other activities. It just really seemed to stand out after that.\n\nDoctor: Was there anything significant happening in your life around the time you took on the extra duties at the community center, aside from the new responsibilities themselves?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Let me think... no, nothing out of the ordinary. It was just the usual day-to-day routine for my husband and me. Taking on those extra duties was really the only big change right around then. Everything else was pretty much exactly the same as always.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn or interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It's actually quite bothersome. Like, if I'm waiting in line at the pharmacy, or even just waiting my turn to speak at one of our volunteer meetings, I get so fidgety. I'll catch myself sighing out loud, or... well, just jumping in and interrupting before the other person is finished. It's hard to just stand there and wait.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty staying focused at home, or is it mainly when you're involved in activities outside the home?\n\nPatient: It happens in both places, honestly. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband... well, my husband and the other volunteers have found they just can’t really count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me. So, yes, it's definitely an issue at the house just as much as when I'm out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Well, not running or climbing, no. I'm a bit past the age for that sort of thing. But I do get very restless. If I have to sit still for too long, I get this... stirred-up feeling inside. It's like I just suddenly need to stand up and straighten something out, or go check on something around the house. I just can't seem to stay put in one spot.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. My kitchen table is actually a pretty good example of that. It's just... well, it's covered in piles right now. I have a pile for my dance class, one for knitting, another for garden events, and then paperwork for trips we're taking. They're just all over the table, and when I sit down to go through them, I... I can’t always figure out what needs to be done first. It's just hard to get it all in order.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. For example, when I help out with the paperwork at the community center... I'll miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I'll write down the wrong room time on a note. And the thing is, I always think I've checked it carefully, but the mistakes still happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as anxiety or depression, alongside these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really felt depressed or overly anxious. It's just the trouble keeping things straight and feeling a bit restless, like I mentioned. But as far as my mood goes, I generally feel fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or suspected to have attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, or any other neurodevelopmental disorder, in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Nobody's ever mentioned anything like that to me before, and I never really thought about it until recently when things just seemed to get a bit harder to manage. But no, no past diagnoses or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with restlessness, staying focused, or completing tasks when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. Even way back in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was a bright girl, but just... scattered. I was always losing my papers, or I'd be talking out of turn in class. And I had a hard time sitting still—I'd be wiggling around in my seat before I even finished my schoolwork. So, yes, it was definitely an issue back then, too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I might bounce around from task to task, but I don't really do things on impulse. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and over at the community center too. \n\nMy husband, and the other volunteers I help out with... well, they just can't really count on me to keep plans straight anymore. Not without someone checking behind me to make sure things are actually getting done. So, yes, it's definitely causing some frustration for all of us.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice this restlessness and difficulty staying focused on one task?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it's been especially noticeable for about... oh, a year and ten months now. That was right around the time I took on the extra coordinating duties at the community center, on top of all my other activities. It's actually quite bothersome. Like, if I'm waiting in line at the pharmacy, or even just waiting my turn to speak at one of our volunteer meetings, I get so fidgety. I'll catch myself sighing out loud, or... well, just jumping in and interrupting before the other person is finished. It's hard to just stand there and wait.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty staying focused at home, or is it mainly when you're involved in activities outside the home?\n\nPatient: It happens in both places, honestly. It's actually started causing some real strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband... well, my husband and the other volunteers have found they just can’t really count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me. So, yes, it's definitely an issue at the house just as much as when I'm out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Well, not running or climbing, no. I'm a bit past the age for that sort of thing. But I do get very restless. If I have to sit still for too long, I get this... stirred-up feeling inside. It's like I just suddenly need to stand up and straighten something out, or go check on something around the house. I just can't seem to stay put in one spot.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. My kitchen table is actually a pretty good example of that. It just seems like it's become more of a noticeable issue for me recently, rather than something I was dealing with that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused consistently since you were a child, or did they seem to lessen at some point before increasing again?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose they must have lessened. Well, no, not throughout my entire life. Like I was saying, there was a long stretch in the middle there where I didn't really notice any of this. I was at home raising the kids, so I didn't have an outside job to worry about... and socially, I always did fine.

But no, it definitely hasn't been a constant problem everywhere I go for my whole life.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid your restlessness and difficulty staying focused start in childhood and continue into adulthood, or did they start later in life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even back in grade school, my teachers told my mother I was bright but scattered, always losing papers, talking out of turn, and wiggling in my seat before I finished my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, they definitely started in childhood. Even way back in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright, but scattered. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and wiggling around in my seat before I even finished my work. 

But as for continuing into adulthood... like I was saying a minute ago, it really didn't. I had that long stretch in the middle where I was just busy raising the family and taking care of the house, and I didn't notice feeling this way at all. It's really just lately that it started back up again.

So, yes, it was definitely an issue back then, too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused consistently throughout your adult life, not just in the past year and ten months?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I mean, I managed fine for most of my adult life. I kept the house running and raised my family, so... if I was restless or having trouble focusing back then, I didn't really notice it. It just seems like it's become more of a noticeable issue for me recently, rather than something I was dealing with that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused consistently since you were a child, or did they seem to lessen at some point before increasing again?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose they must have lessened. Like I was just saying, I really didn't notice feeling this way for most of my adult years. When you're raising a family and taking care of a house, you're just always on the go anyway, so maybe that masked it, or... I don't really know. But I definitely wasn't struggling to stay focused or feeling this restless back then. It really just seems like it faded away after I was a kid and then... well, just started acting up again recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness and difficulty staying focused in multiple settings, such as at work, at home, and in social situations, throughout your entire life?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not throughout my entire life. Like I was saying, there was a long stretch in the middle there where I didn't really notice any of this. I was at home raising the kids, so I didn't have an outside job to worry about... and socially, I always did fine. \n\nIt really just seems like it was an issue when I was little, and now it's happening again at home and when I'm out trying to get my daily things done. But no, it definitely hasn't been a constant problem everywhere I go for my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Did your restlessness and difficulty staying focused start in childhood and continue into adulthood, or did they start later in life?\n\nPatient: Well, they definitely started in childhood. Even way back in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright, but scattered. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and wiggling around in my seat before I even finished my work. \n\nBut as for continuing into adulthood... like I was saying a minute ago, it really didn't. I had that long stretch in the middle where I was just busy raising the family and taking care of the house, and I didn't notice feeling this way at all. It's really just lately that it started back up again.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused been present in some form throughout your entire life, even if they weren't always as noticeable?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no... I really don't think they were. I know you're asking if maybe they were just hiding in the background, but like I've been saying, I just didn't feel this way for decades. When I was raising my kids and keeping up with the house, my mind just wasn't scattered, and I didn't have this restless feeling at all. So... no, I wouldn't say they've been there my whole life. There was a very long break between being a little girl and being the age I am now where I felt completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused ever completely go away for a significant period of time after childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did completely go away. Like I just said, for a really long time—decades, really—I didn't have any of these issues. After I grew up and started my family, the scattered feeling and the restlessness just weren't there anymore. It wasn't until recently that it all came back. So yes, there was a very significant period where they were completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying focused for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. I'd say... well, it's been especially noticeable for about a year and ten months now. That was right around the time I took on the extra coordinating work at the community center, along with all my other activities. So, yes, it's been almost two years since it started acting up again."
